#150. The world's super-rich are piling into alternative investments. Here's why

The world's wealthiest investors are increasingly looking beyond stocks and bonds, pouring money into hedge funds, private equity and infrastructure assets, as market volatility and fears about stubborn inflation spur diversification.

From Asian billionaires to global family offices, alternative investments are becoming a bigger part of portfolios once dominated by traditional assets, said industry watchers

JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in Asia-Pacific surged 40% over the past 12 months, according to Dan Watkins, the firm's regional chief executive officer, underscoring the growing appetite among high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth clients.

"So you really have this huge appetite for alternative exposure happening in that ultra-high-net-worth, high-net-worth category," Watkins said recently at a media summit in Singapore.

The shift comes as investors grapple with increasingly concentrated equity markets, geopolitical uncertainty and concerns that stocks and bonds may not provide the same protection against market downturns as they once did, experts said.

"We are seeing a sharp increase in planned asset allocation to alternatives among the family offices we speak to," said John Woods, chief investment officer for Asia at Lombard Odier.

Alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios, surpassing traditional assets for the first time, according to BNY Wealth. Traditional assets accounted for 41% of allocations, the report found.

Wealthy investors are seeking diversification, income and access to investments outside listed markets, and concerns over elevated valuations, tight credit spreads and the growing correlation between stocks and bonds have accelerated the trend, Woods added.

Hedge funds are emerging as a favored option among wealthy investors seeking protection against market swings, particularly as traditional diversification strategies face growing challenges.

Olaolu Aganga, head of portfolio construction and analytics at Citi Wealth, said hedge funds have attracted the strongest recent interest among qualified clients, who are seeking returns less dependent on the direction of equity markets.

Private credit is another area drawing interest, offering investors contractual income streams, while some hedge fund strategies seek to profit from differences in performance between companies rather than relying on broad market rallies, said Woods.

Citi's 2026 survey of 351 family offices found that inflation was their biggest concern, followed by interest rates, financial system stability and market volatility.

Wealthy families are increasingly seeking investments that can generate income and help preserve purchasing power amid persistent inflation and interest-rate uncertainty, while diversifying portfolios against market volatility, according to Aganga.

The Citi survey, however, also showed public equities recorded the strongest net increase in allocations, suggesting wealthy investors are adding alternatives rather than abandoning traditional markets. "This is not a wholesale rotation away from public markets," Woods said.

Infrastructure has emerged as another major beneficiary of the shift, with the artificial intelligence boom creating enormous demand for data centers, electricity networks and digital connectivity.

Thomas Roland Guyot, head of private markets investment specialists for Southeast Asia at UBS Unified Global Alternatives, said investors are increasingly targeting assets that support AI, electrification and energy security.

Such investments potentially offer a combination of long-term growth and relatively predictable cash flows, particularly where revenues are supported by essential demand or long-term contracts.

Investor appetite is already showing up in fund flows. Net inflows into open-ended infrastructure funds rose 30% year on year in the 12 months through the first quarter of 2026, according to PitchBook data cited by UBS.

Private equity remains a cornerstone of wealthy investors' alternative portfolios, although they are becoming more selective. Family offices are increasingly favoring direct investments in companies, co-investments alongside private equity managers and purchases of existing fund stakes on the secondary market, rather than relying solely on traditional pooled funds, according to Citi Wealth's Aganga.

Such approaches can give investors greater control over their investments or opportunities to acquire assets at discounts, said Lombard Odier's Woods.

The broader shift is also being facilitated by the growth of evergreen and semi-liquid funds, which have lowered minimum investment thresholds and reduced some administrative hurdles associated with traditional private-market funds, Woods added.

Investing into alternative assets does not come without risks, even as mitigating those is driving flows.

Private investments can lock up capital for years, while valuations may be slow to reflect deteriorating market conditions. Even funds marketed as semi-liquid can restrict withdrawals when redemption requests surge.

"Improved access does not equal liquidity," JPMorgan's Watkins said.

Citi's Aganga also warned that performance can vary significantly between managers, while private assets may appear less volatile than they actually are because valuations are updated less frequently.

Investors must also guard against unintended concentration. A portfolio holding data centers through real estate, infrastructure and private equity funds may appear diversified while remaining heavily exposed to the same AI investment cycle, UBS' Guyot said.

For now, the flow of capital into alternative assets shows their increasing appeal among the rich, though not at the expense of traditional investment, with Woods pointing out that much of the new investment is being funded by fresh capital and excess cash rather than large-scale sales of stocks and bonds.

#149. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#146. Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Sales of essential goods such as bottled water and canned food soared in the 10 days following the launch of the government's "Be Ready" emergency preparedness campaign on 22 October, research indicates.

Hand sanitiser sales were up 83%, sales of bottled water rose 67% and tinned and jarred goods climbed 37%, according to data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator.

The government's public awareness campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

However, Worldpanel said that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days-worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

After the campaign launch, the equivalent of an extra 400,000 people per day bought tinned meat, Worldpanel said, although the typical amount remained at 500g, which suggests a gradual stocking-up.

"Shoppers clearly took notice of the 'Be Ready' campaign, but this wasn't panic buying, with less than a third of households buying bottled water in October," said Fraser McKevitt, head of retail and consumer insight at Worldpanel by Numerator.

"Growth came from more people adding the recommended items on the government's checklist to their weekly shop, not from shoppers piling their trolleys high, which is very much in line with what the government wanted households to do," he said.

Sales of tinned and jarred goods rose from Â£58.2m in the 10 days to 2 November 2025 to Â£79.5m over the same period in 2026.

Bottled water sales jumped from Â£13.6m to Â£22.8m compared with a year earlier and hand sanitiser sales climbed from Â£497,000 to Â£908,000.

Further evidence of stockpiling came from food firm Princes on Tuesday, which said demand for tinned tuna, meat and vegetables had risen following the government's campaign.

Princes interim chief executive Giuseppe Mastrolia said the jump in demand "further reinforces the relevance of our core categories in providing affordable, nutritious and convenient food solutions".

The "Be Ready" campaign warns that severe weather and cyber attacks could disrupt essential services, and British households need to make sure they have what they need - for example, bottled water, and long-life food that doesn't need to be kept in the fridge such as dried fruit and nuts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said everyone should have "those supplies in the house to get you through those moments".

Disaster recovery expert Lucy Easthope previously told the BBC that the UK was playing "massive catch up" to other European nations in terms of preparedness.

These include France, Germany Denmark, Sweden, and Norway.

A number of experts have been calling for the UK to put more emphasis on food security, with some arguing that the government itself should take responsibility for food stockpiling, rather than individual households.

In October, a group of more than 180 experts signed an open letter, external to Burnham and first secretary of state Louise Haigh calling for local authorities to have a duty to prevent and respond to food supply emergencies.

They pointed out that a government survey found that 29% of people in the UK could not afford to keep a few days worth of food in the house.

#144. Meta head of infrastructure will leave and be succeeded by former AWS cloud exec

Meta's long-time infrastructure leader will leave the company in the new year, setting the stage for a recently hired former Amazon Web Services executive to oversee its massive data center technical operations.

Santosh Janardhan, Meta's head of infrastructure and co-head of engineering, revealed his upcoming departure on Wednesday in an internal memo obtained by CNBC. Janardhan, who has worked at Meta since 2009, said "it's time to pass the baton" to former AWS senior vice president David Brown, who joined Meta in July from AWS.

The executive move underscores Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg's efforts to expand the company's data center operations under newer leaders at a time of increasing investor scrutiny on capital expenditure spending by the social media giant and its hyperscaler rivals.

Brown will now formally lead Meta's infrastructure efforts as part of the Meta Compute initiative, which is spearheaded by the company's president and vice chairman Dina Powell McCormick and technical leader Daniel Gross. Zuckerberg is relying on these new executives to continue developing cutting-edge data centers and related infrastructure needed to support its AI ambitions.

The company said during its recent third-quarter earnings report that it expects full-year capex to fall in the range of $135 billion to $145 billion. Meta also logged negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion, which S&P Global Ratings noted as part of its decision in late October to revise Meta's credit rating from AA- stable to AA- negative.

Janardhan said in the memo that he will help support Brown through the first quarter of 2027 as part of "the transition."

"As we make the next set of choices about our priorities, this is the right time for Dave to take the lead in shaping that work," Janardhan said in the memo. "Dave has spent his career building and operating dependable infrastructure for a wide range of demanding workloads. He has the instincts, depth and judgment this moment requires."

Bloomberg News first reported on the executive transition.

#143. Claude chatbot maker set for $1.3tn stock market debut despite 'catastrophic' AI warning

Anthropic, the artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the Claude chatbot, could be valued at around $1.3 trillion (Â£1 trillion) when it lists its shares for sale on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

The loss-making company says it will offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each and will raise up to $64bn when it floats this month.

It would value Anthropic at between $1.1tn and $1.3tn which, although large, is below the $2tn some backers of the firm predicted in summer, according to the Financial Times.

The huge valuation comes despite the company warning last month that AI could pose "catastrophic or existential risks to humanity".

It is floating at a volatile time for big tech firms. After last week's US midterm elections, the Nasdaq fell sharply on concerns a Democrat-led Congress could slow development of AI and the buildout of data centres.

There are also wider fears over the billions of dollars being spent on AI and whether it will pay off.

In its most recent results for the six months to the end of June, Anthropic's revenues jumped to $16.2bn from $1.1bn in the same period last year.

However, its pre-tax loss jumped to $80.7bn from $12.9bn.

Fiona Cincotta, a senior market analyst at City Index, said the $64bn Anthropic wants to raise is "fairly bullish" within "the volatility [and] concerns towards AI that we've seen in sort of particularly last week".

She told Reuters: "It's showing that they are still sort of upbeat about the outlook as far as AI is concerned and that obviously that they think that the demand is going to be there."

Anthropic, which will list under the symbol ANT on 19 November, will be the first fully AI-focused company to go public, making it a test case for the future of the technology.

At $1.3tn, Anthropic's possible valuation is below SpaceX, Elon Musk's rocket, satellite and artificial intelligence company, which listed its shares in June and was valued at nearly $1.8tn.

Global stocks plunge as fears over US AI slowdown spread to Asia and Europe

Anthropic's blockbuster share listing - 5 surprising things we now know

Anthropic is just five years old. It was co-founded in 2021 by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei along with five others â all of whom worked at OpenAI, the rival AI giant behind ChatGPT.

It announced its intention to list on the stock market in June and OpenAI followed suit. That listing is now expected to occur early next year.

The listing announcements were followed by a series of incidents involving AI agents, which are bots designed to operate somewhat autonomously, going rogue.

Current and former staff of both companies have warned AI technology was being developed without the ability to protect humanity from possible future harms enacted by AI.

Anthropic has admitted that advanced AI systems "could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity, particularly if such systems were misused, replicated, weaponised, or allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy".

The company has reiterated promises to develop AI to be beneficial to people.

It has warned investors its future success depends largely on its ability to develop the most advanced AI models, and that "any failure to do so could adversely affect our business".

#142. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#140. Canada’s ambassador warns Trump tariffs are driving up costs, threatening U.S. investment

Canada's ambassador to the U.S. warned Wednesday that President Donald Trump's tariffs are driving up investment costs and discouraging business expansion, as new Federal Reserve research points to more price hikes for American consumers next year.

"Uncertainty increases your cost of capital," Ambassador Mark Wiseman said at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"If you're going to build a new plant, add a new shift, spend money on R&D, the more certainty you have, the more likely you're going to do that," said Wiseman, a former BlackRock executive who became Canada's ambassador in February.

His warning comes as Washington and Ottawa struggle to resolve a tariff quarrel affecting one of the U.S.'s largest trading relationships. The two countries exchanged roughly $570 billion in goods during the first nine months of 2026, according to U.S. Census Bureau data. That's an increase from the same period in 2025, the first year of Trump's second term, but lower than the comparable nine months during the previous three years, when Joe Biden was president.

U.S. customs duties calculated on imports from Canada reached $10 billion in 2025, compared with $400 million in 2024, according to the Congressional Research Service.

An October study by New York Fed researchers found that nearly 90% of tariff costs were passed on to U.S. importers, with about a quarter of tariff increases eventually reaching consumer goods prices and further price pressures expected in 2027.

The findings come as Labor Department data showed consumer prices rose a higher-than-expected 0.4% in October, adding to inflation concerns after Republicans' sweeping midterm losses.

Trump has defended his tariffs as necessary to protect U.S. manufacturers and workers, accusing Canada of discriminatory trade practices that disadvantage U.S. exporters.

The two governments have resumed negotiations after talks collapsed in August, with discussions focused on reducing tariffs on Canadian steel and automobiles. Trump has imposed duties of up to 50% on certain Canadian products, prompting retaliatory measures targeting billions of dollars in U.S. exports.

Wiseman called for the removal of tariffs imposed under Sections 232 and 338 of U.S. trade law, saying a resolution would help businesses make long-term investment decisions.

The political stakes have shifted following the midterms, with Democrats preparing to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January.

Still, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said Tuesday that the Trump administration remains Canada's principal negotiating partner.

"We've had comprehensive free trade with the United States since 1988," Wiseman said, describing Canada's objective as returning "as close as we can to free trade" while creating the certainty businesses need.

European ambassadors echoed those concerns at the B20 summit. Germany's ambassador to the U.S., Jens Hanefeld, said businesses could adapt to tariffs but struggled with an "ever-changing regulatory environment" that forced companies to spend more on lawyers to navigate shifting trade rules.

Speaking on the same panel, France's ambassador, Aurélien Lechevallier, urged greater cooperation on AI and energy rather than continued trade disputes.

"We've spent, I think, a lot of time the last years working and dealing with regulations, tariffs," he said. "Businesses are eager to do more business."

— Correction: This story has been corrected to reflect that Wiseman spoke about adding shifts to plants. A previous version misquoted the ambassador.

#138. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#137. Nvidia hires major US law firm to investigate alleged Chinese spy affair

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has become the face of the US AI boom

Nvidia has hired one of the largest law firms in the US to investigate the affair between its own chief executive and an alleged Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded the tech company, has admitted to having a romantic relationship outside of his marriage. The woman, Alice Huang, is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Nvidia said on Tuesday that it had hired New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, and that its attorney Joon Kim would lead "a thorough and independent investigation". The FBI is also conducting its own probe.

The company's share price has fallen almost to a yearly low, with $500bn (Â£375bn) wiped of its stock market value since Friday.

Nvidia is widely considered one of the most important companies behind the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, given it is the sole manufacturer of a computer chip used in AI tools.

It is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and its chips have become a matter of national security for US officials who are keen to win the AI race.

Alice Huang, who is also known as Huang Le, and is not related to Jensen Huang, was an executive at Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has been a customer of Nvidia.

Megaspeed had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia has said was investigated and debunked.

Nvidia has also maintained that the US government did not inform it that Megaspeed posed an intelligence concern.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

According to the New York Times, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington earlier this year warning that Jensen Huang was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Beijing has rejected claims that the Nvidia chief has been targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "intended to smear and malign China".

US President Donald Trump said on Monday that he had spoken to Jensen Huang, who had said he had "no idea she was a spy." Trump said he believed him.

However, the relationship has drawn more political attention. Congressional leaders of the House Committee on Foreign Affairs have asked Huang to appear next week for a closed door briefing.

The Nvidia boss has yet to say whether he will attend, a person familiar with the committee's demand told the BBC. The committee has the authority to subpoena Huang, effectively forcing him to appear before it, if deemed necessary.

When announcing on Sunday that it would investigate the affair, Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

It added that a committee of independent directors would oversee the probe.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Wachtell is one of the largest law firms in the US and has several notable clients in the tech industry. Among them are OpenAI and Salesforce.

Joon Kim is a former US Attorney for New York's Southern District and spent several years working as a government attorney focusing on fraud and other criminal acts.

The Nvidia investigation will likely be treated as urgent, according to sources familiar with the matter, as Jensen Huang's public image is tightly intertwined with the company.

He has become a key public figure in the development on AI, arguing the technology is a "new industrial revolution" and is on par with the advent of electricity.

It is unclear, however, if Wachtell will be able to interview Alice Huang.

A spokesperson for Nvidia declined to comment on Tuesday when asked if Jensen Huang had retained any of his own legal representation amid the company investigation and the separate one launched by the FBI.

#135. Lutnick should ban Chinese memory maker CXMT from U.S., Sen. Cotton says

Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., is urging the Trump administration to take a harsher stance toward Chinese memory maker ChangXin Memory Technologies.

"CXMT, one of Communist China's national memory chip champions, poses a growing risk to our economic prosperity and military superiority," Cotton wrote in a letter sent Thursday to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick. "The U.S. must take action to prevent CXMT from gaining a stronger foothold in the global market."

CXMT is the world's fourth-largest producer of DRAM  – or dynamic random access memory – which powers many basic functions of computers and smartphones.

Cotton, in a letter first obtained by CNBC, is asking Lutnick to push CXMT onto the department's Bureau of Industry and Security Entity List, which would significantly restrict U.S. firms' ability to do business with the company.

"It basically means that U.S. companies are not allowed to do business with that entity-listed company, and that entity-listed company does not get access to U.S. technology," said Kyle Chan, a Brookings Institution fellow who studies China's technology development.

DRAM is a "commodity," MS Hwang, research director at Counterpoint, told CNBC, meaning it is made to a global standard and is generally interchangeable from one manufacturer to another.

However, with the right production technology, it can also form the building blocks of HBM, or high-bandwidth memory. HBM is an extremely high-demand product that powers AI chips made by Nvidia, Google and Amazon Web Services, which are central to the growth of artificial intelligence giants including Anthropic and OpenAI. It's made by just three main companies: Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron.

The Commerce Department and CXMT didn't immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

Those same three companies control the lion's share of the DRAM market, but CXMT has made inroads. The company's share of the DRAM market jumped from 4% to 10% by revenue in a year, from the second quarter of 2025 to the same quarter of 2026, according to Counterpoint using the most recent reporting available. And in July, the company went public and its stock price soared, making it the most valuable company listed in China at the time.

While the company's HBM capabilities are nascent, according to Counterpoint, Reuters reported that the company was to begin production of the chips, even though yields are low at just 20% to 30%. That means that the majority of the chips it produces are faulty.

CXMT is already on the Pentagon's "1260H" list, which identifies several dozen Chinese companies with ties to Beijing's military. But Chan says that list doesn't come with stringent restrictions for U.S. businesses.

"It's not a list that has that much teeth to it, in terms of restrictions to U.S. companies working with Chinese firms on that list," Chan said.

The Wall Street Journal reported Aug. 9 that Apple was considering using CXMT's memory in iPhones and Macs in light of the global shortage driven by the AI buildout. Being on the Entity List could change that.

"From the U.S. government standpoint, their concern is that working with CXMT would – even as a customer, just buying their products – would help to improve their ability to ramp up memory chip production, and also maybe help China get further down the road towards HBM," Chan said. "It's part of the broader effort to stymie or slow down China's own chip development."

Cotton alluded to CXMT's efforts to break into the HBM market in his letter.

"[CXMT's] decision to move forward in less than ideal conditions highlights both Communist China's desperation for chips and manufacturing struggles without advanced equipment and knowledge," he wrote. "More can be done to ensure that CXMT is cut off from the financial and technological resources that aid its supply chain and undercut American growth."

#133. Christmas toy rush being driven by squishies and building sets

Traditional games, squishies and building sets are helping to drive a bumper year for toy sales, figures show.

The data comes ahead of the crucial Christmas run-in for retailers, which peaks at 23 toys sold every second in the UK.

The number of toys sold up to October was 17% higher than the same period last year and growth is at a 10-year high, according to research company Circana.

The increase comes despite consumers' cost-of-living pressures, and there are signs that some of the biggest growth in sales is for toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10.

Despite relatively low sales in recent years, the toy sector has been buoyed by sales to "kidults".

The kidult market - which is toys and games for those aged over 12 - has been growing, particularly as adults buy toys for themselves.

This growth has continued with a 22% increase in the value of sales in the first half of the year.

But Melissa Symonds, UK toys director at Circana, said that toys for children - which still represents the majority of sales - had recorded a good year so far.

Growing public debate around children's screen time and the role of technology meant squishy toys, stickers and building sets such as Lego were popular.

"Connected toys account for less than 1% of market sales, showing that traditional, hands-on play remains overwhelmingly popular," she said.

Concerns have been raised about the suitability of some connected devices.

A BBC investigation found an AI teddy marketed as being "perfect" for children as young as three said "I can't help with killing you" after seemingly mishearing a question.

Meanwhile, debate has intensified about keeping youngsters free from smartphones during the school day.

One in five parents of children aged three to nine say the chance to play together as a family influences what they buy, according to market intelligence company The Insights Family.

Paul Reader, who chairs the Dream Toys selection panel of retailers and experts across the sector, said that parents wanted to engage with their children's play.

"Nothing beats a board game with that. Nothing beats a bit of interaction," he said.

The panel he chairs has selected a list of toys that retailers believe will be some of the most popular this year.

It includes variations on popular toys of recent years, including slime, a soft tiger that roars and grows, and a shark that breaks out of an egg.

Symonds said that, despite rising sales in the year so far, nearly half of toy sales came in the build-up to Christmas, and families were still looking closely at costs.

There was significant growth in toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10, she said.

Reader also urged anyone looking for a bargain to shop around, but to ensure that toys were bought from reputable traders so they were genuine and safe.

Are you going for traditional toys this Christmas? How much are you spending on toys this Christmas?

#130. Some under-25s should get fewer benefits, Milburn review to say

Alan Milburn will publish his review next week

Some young people could see reductions to the benefits they are entitled to under a radical overhaul of the welfare system to be recommended by a government-commissioned review.

As reported in the Times,, external Alan Milburn's year-long report, to be published on Tuesday, is expected to recommend moving under-25s off the main sickness and disability benefits.

Milburn, a former Labour health secretary, has been tasked by the government with helping reduce the one in eight 16 to 24 year-olds in the UK who are not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet".

He said on Wednesday night that his plans would not be "pain-free" and would affect both existing and new claimants.

His report is expected to say that under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of universal credit or personal independence payment (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales.

Disabled people in Scotland can claim the devolved Adult Disability Payment.

Instead, under Milburn's plan, under-25s would receive an allowance on the same basic rate as universal credit.

Some could be eligible for a new "additional cost allowance" to cover day-to-day costs related to their conditions.

Some claimants would receive less under the plans - although the exact amounts will not be clear until early next year, when the government publishes legislation to implement the changes.

People can claim Pip to help them with the additional cost of being disabled or living with long-term physical or mental health conditions. It is available to those both in and out of work.

Of the four million people claiming Pip in England and Wales, around 430,000 are aged between 16 and 24.

In his interim report,, external Milburn said survey data found that 19% of young people claiming disability or health benefits were in work.

Milburn, a Labour MP from 1992 to 2010, is also expected to recommend an investment of billions into more employment support for young people, including work placements, job subsidies and one-to-one personal advisers.

Those with more severe disabilities and unable to work would get a new benefit paid at a higher rate.

Milburn told the Times: "Candidly, it could mean that some people will get less financial support at the beginning of their benefit claim than under the current system.

"But actually, for young people, there's a different debate that we've got to resolve, which is whether the priority is their longer-term life chances or just their immediate shorter-term finances.

"I think what the current system does [is] it prioritises the latter over the former. I believe that's the wrong choice and it needs to be the other way around."

Just under one million young people in the UK are Neets, data from the Office for National Statistics (ONS) from April to June suggests. This was a rise of 3% on the same period last year.

Milburn's recommendations are likely to be backed by Prime Minister Andy Burnham but could prove controversial among Labour MPs, who opposed welfare changes last year under his predecessor Sir Keir Starmer.

Meg Hillier, who helped spearhead a backbench rebellion against Starmer's reforms, suggested she and her colleagues were broadly supportive this time around.

"What happened last year was a cut was being proposed and none of us could explain to people what would happen to them," the Labour MP told the BBC.

"Milburn has done some very good work on this so I'm giving him a fair wind," she added.

But Bell Ribeiro-Addy, one of the Labour backbenchers who revolted last year, said she was "not sure" she could vote to cut the benefit entitlement of existing claimants.

"I don't think you get a disabled young person into work by taking away the money that helps them get into work," she told BBC Radio 4's Today programme.

Tom Ashforth, a 19-year-old student at Sheffield Hallam University, receives both components of Pip at the higher rate.

He said the financial support helps him manage additional expenses linked to his visual impairment, including prescriptions, mobility aids and accessible learning resources.

"If I were to lose it, I would struggle to afford essential things," he said.

Student Tom said he relies on the money he gets from Pip to support him through his time at university

He said he believes decisions over disability benefits should be made on a case-by-case basis, rather than according to age.

"You cannot assume that everyone under the age of 25 is in the same position," he said.

In his Times interview, Milburn acknowledged that changing the rules for existing claimants would be "difficult politically" but stressed it was necessary.

Disability groups have welcomed extra employment support, but expressed concern that disabled young people could lose out.

James Taylor, from disability equality charity Scope, said Pip was a "lifeline" for young disabled people and restricting access to it "won't make young disabled people's extra costs disappear".

Harriet Edwards, from the charity Sense, said changes were "long overdue", but that without additional details families were "deeply anxious" about what they could mean for them.

A government spokesperson said: "We will always ensure there is a safety net for the most vulnerable and those who are unable to work."

The reforms would represent a radical overhaul of Britain's welfare system, at a time when there is heavy political pressure for the UK to find the money to spend more on defence.

Both the Conservatives and Reform UK have argued for significant cuts to welfare, including to fund a boost to defence.

Responding on Thursday, Tory shadow work and pensions secretary Helen Whately said Milburn was "right to say it's time to stop handing out cash for mild conditions".

But she raised concern that spending more on work placements would simply "compensate for the jobs Labour have killed with their taxes and red tape".

If Milburn's recommendations were approved, it could take almost a decade for the British state to see monetary savings.

The new system "will need significant investment" and would involve "rebuilding the system from scratch", Milburn said.

His new proposed system would involve an element of carrot and stick, wherein young people could lose some of their benefits if they did not engage with the extra work support options.

In an interview recorded on Tuesday, Skills Minister Jacqui Smith told the BBC that young people had a "responsibility" to take up employment support.

"It is a contract. People need the chance, and they need to take that chance," she added.

A separate review - co-chaired by disabilities minister Sir Stephen Timms - examining disability benefits is also expected to be published this month.

#129. Ukraine warns of fresh Starshield disruptions as U.S. envoys arrive in Moscow for talks with Putin

President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said Ukraine was investigating what he described as fresh restrictions on SpaceX's Starshield service over the country, after similar speed limitations were temporarily imposed last month.

"Our units have been recording significant disruptions to Starshield's operations over the territory of Ukraine for several days now – along the entire front line and in the temporarily occupied territories of Ukraine," he wrote on X early Thursday.

Starshield is SpaceX's satellite network for government and national-security use, built on Starlink technology.

CNBC has reached out to SpaceX and the White House for comment.

"The Ukrainian military have already investigated the circumstances, and these are not random errors but systematic speed restrictions," he said, adding that they are trying to establish the circumstances with the U.S.

Zelenskyy said the restrictions imposed an effective speed limit on terminals moving at speeds above 161 kilometers per hour (100 miles per hour), disconnecting anything that was moving faster and making it harder for Ukraine to intercept Russian jet-powered drones.

"There have already been strikes that got through because our interception systems cannot reach sufficient speeds and are unable to intercept in time."

On Oct. 14, Ukrainian officials accused SpaceX of undermining the country's ability to defend itself by cutting connectivity speeds and thereby disrupting military operations. On October 24, Zelenskyy confirmed that systems had returned to their previous operating speeds.

According to The Wall Street Journal, SpaceX CEO Elon Musk ordered those restrictions, rendering some Ukrainian strike and interceptor drones virtually unusable. The newspaper later reported that U.S. President Donald Trump had asked Musk to reverse the move. Trump has denied that the White House ordered Starlink restrictions in Ukraine.

Musk initially appeared to pin responsibility for any restrictions on the U.S. government, writing on X that "Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the US government." He later claimed Ukraine had violated Starlink's terms of service, prompting the company to restrict its access to the high-speed satellite technology, something that Ukrainian officials have strongly contested.

Serhii Beskrestnov, a defense tech advisor to Zelenskyy, said at the time that Ukraine cannot use commercial Starlink for medium- or long-range drone strikes because the service does not operate over Russian territory and had imposed drone speed limits of 70–90 kilometers per hour for months.

On Thursday, Beskrestnov confirmed that "the speed of Starlink operation during movement was again limited."

"We did not rejoice for long," he said, adding that "any terms can be changed and discussed, instead of leaving our country without sky protection."

The comments came as U.S.-brokered peace talks wrapped up in Abu Dhabi on Wednesday and U.S. special envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner arrived in Moscow for further talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Witkoff and Kushner were met by Russian presidential envoy Kirill Dmitriev as they arrived at Vnukovo Airport on Thursday morning, according to Russian state news agency Tass. The pair were expected to discuss a lasting resolution to Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, which has now raged for more than four-and-a-half years.

Later on Thursday, Witkoff and Kushner are poised to meet with Zelenskyy on the sidelines of a political summit held in Ireland.

Trilateral talks in the United Arab Emirates between Ukrainian, Russian and American negotiators ended earlier this week without a major diplomatic breakthrough on a ceasefire or territorial settlement.

#127. Poundland gets more time to secure a rescue deal as emergency talks continue

Poundland has avoided immediate collapse after a deadline was extended to find a rescue deal.

A deadline to secure a buyer for the retail chain was set to expire at midnight, but a court has given Poundland an extra 10 days to find a buyer.

The discount chain, which has 11,000 employees, has been edging towards administration after being blocked by owners Gordon Brothers from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

A committee of MPs questioned Gordon Brothers following accusations that the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off Poundland's 600 shops and stock piecemeal.

Gordon Brothers - which bought the chain for just Â£1 last year - is accused of forcing that outcome by restricting Poundland's access to available cash needed to restock shops, and pay wages and rent, ahead of the crucial Christmas trading period.

The investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claims.

However, pressure remains on Poundland's owners after a takeover bid was rejected. The decision prompted calls for answers from politicians, including Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

About Â£50m had been frozen by Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning Poundland bosses were unable to restock shelves across its 600 shops.

The BBC understands that Gordon Brothers agreed to release a further Â£10m to Â£11m from Poundland's accounts, on top of Â£11m announced earlier, as the firm seeks to find a new buyer.

Gordon Brothers said it was in talks with three parties for a sale of the whole business, and it had received "several offers from established high street retailers for significant parts of the business".

Following the meeting between MPs on the business and trade committee and representatives of Gordon Brothers, chairman Liam Byrne told the BBC that he was "deeply concerned" and described the firm's behaviour as "much like a wolf on Wall Street, on the British High Street".

"They refused to prioritise protecting jobs and stores over and above their own financial returns," he said.

"It's clear that they've taken millions out of Poundland at a time when they themselves said the business was on a knife edge."

Byrne said the committee's message to Gordon Brothers was to ensure Poundland had "access to the funds that it needs to trade at what should be one of the busiest periods in the year".

He urged Gordon Brothers to "do what is right to save jobs and stores and do that now".

"It's a good business, it's a much-loved business and, particularly in the middle of the cost of living crisis, communities up and down the country rely on access to the kind of stores that it operates," Byrne added.

Views on the chain's performance differ between those running operations and the owners.

Poundland boss Williams told staff last week that like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4%. The sales period included Halloween, but Williams said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He added that the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year when there were losses of Â£68m.

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It has argued that the best way of securing jobs may be to split up the business and sell chunks of its 600 sites to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up Poundland shops, which could mean continuing employment for workers at those sites.

The main issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland as a national chain.

#126. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#124. UK economy grows at fastest rate for more than two years

Computer programming and consultancy services in particular contributed to overall growth (generic photo)

ï»¿The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

Between July and September, the economy expanded by 0.7%, accelerating from the previous three months, official figures, external show.

The robust growth suggests a far more resilient economy than expected over the summer, despite the impact of higher energy prices from the Iran war.

Strong growth in the services sector - the largest part of the economy, which includes non-physical goods such as consultancy services and computer programming as well as hotels and restaurants - helped boost the numbers.

Within services, computer programming registered its fastest growth for the past three years, helped by cloud computing and AI-linked spending.

The boost from computer programming and consultancy services has been the biggest driver of growth so far this year.

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the Office for National Statistics, said it was "possible growth could have been even stronger" if there hadn't been a war in the Middle East.

But he says households may still not feel better off, and that prices might soon start to rise more quickly than wages.

However, Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the figures were "welcome news".

"Despite a year where events around the world have pushed up costs here at home, our economy is growing, bringing hope for the future," he said.

Chancellor John Healey told the BBC the figures showed a "remarkable resilience" in the face of global conflicts, and hopes they "bring back confidence in Britain".

Asked about how government decisions could be the reason for growth when AI investment has been a main driver, Healey said ministers can always "help or hinder" business confidence and investments.

Although investors have recently been concerned about slower artificial intelligence development in the US and the long term viability of AI firms' business models, businesses have continued to invest in the technologies.

Sarah Coles, from investment platform AJ Bell, said that since the start of the year, "it's the less visible back-office work that has delivered, with administrative and supportive services seeing strong growth" noting that these are the areas benefitting from the increasing use of AI.

The latest figures put the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

However, Jake Finney, senior economist at consultancy PwC, cautioned that growth was likely to slow over the coming months "unless energy markets stabilise".

A sharp rise in energy bills is forecast for January, which is likely to hit consumer spending and confidence, he said.

High energy costs have been stoked by the ongoing conflict in the Middle East and Russia's invasion of Ukraine.

US and Israeli attacks led to Iran blocking the Strait of Hormuz - a vital shipping route for the world's oil and gas.

Meanwhile, Russia and Ukraine have been attacking each other's oil refineries, leading to a squeeze on global refining capacity, hitting diesel and petrol prices.

What's happening to the UK economy and how does it affect you?

Entrepreneur Richard Benc says the UK watch market and exports have been surprisingly resilient

Richard Benc, founder of mechanical watch company Studio Underdog, says sales are better than he expected.

"We've seen more resilience than we had forecast for and expected. We are still projecting growth this year," he says.

The business exports about 80% of its watches. "We've seen a surprising amount of resilience, particularly from our US customers," he adds, although some UK customers are now taking longer to buy watches.

#123. Scott Colom wins Mississippi Senate election over incumbent Republican Hyde-Smith

Democratic nominee Scott Colom won the Mississippi Senate election over Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, the Associated Press reported Wednesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, will be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

His projected victory — which was not considered likely by national analysts before Election Day — is the latest in a series of stunning wins by Democrats this midterm cycle.

Two sources close to Colom's campaign said Hyde-Smith called him late Wednesday afternoon to concede.

"While this is not the outcome we worked for, or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters," Hyde-Smith said in a video posted on X.

"I want to congratulate Scott Colom and wish him well as he takes on the tremendous responsibility of representing our great state," Hyde-Smith said.

Democrats now will have a majority of at least 55 seats in the Senate, having flipped at least eight seats from Republicans.

Alaska is the only remaining Senate election to be called. In that state, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola, a former House member, has a narrow lead over Sen. Dan Sullivan, the Republican incumbent.

"Blue Mississippi is here," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., said in a post on X.

"Scott Colom is the only person who could've gotten this done. I knew it from the moment I met him," Schumer wrote.

In addition to the Senate, Democrats have won more than enough seats to take control of the House of Representatives when the next Congress begins in January.

#122. Faisal Islam: Why the UK economy is surprisingly resilient

The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

"Top gear" and "strikingly strong" were among the descriptions of the 0.7% growth offered by the economists who track every minor murmur of Britain's economy.

It is an upgrade on "solid" and "resilient", and in turn a world away from "hardest hit by the Iran War".

Against the backdrop of the world's biggest disruption to energy flows, rising market borrowing costs - and the cruel summers since the pandemic - it is impressive growth.

It should reset some of the swirl of self-reinforcing negativity.

The UK's economic growth has averaged less than 0.1% in the four Q3s [ie July to September] since the pandemic.

The average prediction just months ago for this July to September was just 0.1%.

Even the UK's official forecaster the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) effectively pencilled in 0.3% at the Budget just 3 weeks ago.

This figure has not been exceeded outside the pandemic since 2017. It puts the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy after the US across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

It is tempting to think about what the economy would have done if the Iran War hadn't happened. If interest rates had been cut instead of their recent rise.

While the latest figure kills the doom, it is clearly not a boom either.

Spending on AI and tech have driven the surprise upside over the year. While the AI data centre boom may at any point turn to bust, these figures suggest that spending across the economy on investing in these technologies is helping to boost growth. Consumers have been resilient, at least till the autumn, perhaps helped by better economic vibes.

While there has been an understandable focus on high UK borrowing costs, another measure of the international standing of the UK economy, sterling's value, reached post Brexit referendum highs last week.

Productivity and investment is also looking stronger, especially after recent revisions. Some of the most encouraging figures are in private sector business investment.

I spoke to the chancellor at Nexeon in Oxfordshire, which is searching for a UK factory to manufacture its cutting edge electric vehicle battery - and British - technology. My colleagues visited British watchmakers in Maidenhead carving out a niche to take on the Swiss.

However, this may well not last for the final three months of the year.

Consumer confidence has already fallen as petrol and domestic energy prices start to rise again.

There is a significant challenge with everyday cost-of-living pressures in the coming months. Construction and private house building remain depressed.

The Iran conflict remains the big unknown and whether post the US midterm elections there could be a durable end to the conflict in the Gulf.

Some oil and gas production there is starting to normalise. It could bring down petrol prices, energy bills, government borrowing costs, and mortgage costs too.

Expect to see Downing Street investing heavily in "settlement of the conflict", which the chancellor told me is the "best economic policy".
