#120. Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors

Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.

The chief executive and co-founder of the AI chip-making giant is at the centre of a major scandal over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

On Thursday, Nvidia's board announced he had resigned effective immediately.

He will be replaced, for now, by Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress.

The tech giant is facing one of its biggest challenges, finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.

We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors.

Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia

Nvidia's finance chief, Colette Kress, is taking on the chief executive role for an interim period while the company searches for a permanent replacement.

An industry veteran for more than 30 years, Kress previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.

Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.

She "fronts every earnings call" alongside Huang and is a "familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one," said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.

But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.

Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders

Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.

Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.

Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.

Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.

"He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.

Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.

Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.

Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.

Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts.

Liu said Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair.

Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House

Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chipmaking giant.

Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.

Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana.

"The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry," Liu said.

Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.

"The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct," she said. "It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies."

Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.

Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that "our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here".

Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: "We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines."

Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.

"That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen," he said.

#119. As credit card balances hit a record high, medical bills can become 'shadow debt,' expert says

Total household debt rose $334 billion in the third quarter, to $19.1 trillion, according to new data from the Federal Reserve Bank of New York. That total doesn't include medical debt — at least, not directly.

An estimated $220 billion in medical debt exists, according to an analysis of 2021 federal survey data by KFF, a national nonprofit healthcare research group. But medical expenses can become hard-to-track "shadow" debt if consumers pay them with a credit card or other forms of borrowing, said Jim Triggs, the CEO of Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling organization.

"Technically, as soon as that consumer pays that debt off with another mechanism, now it becomes credit card debt, or housing debt, or whatever mechanism you used to pay it off," Triggs said.

He said 20% of MMI's debt and budget counseling clients in 2025 cited a medical hardship as the reason they went into debt.

Separately, about 17% of households surveyed in 2024 reported still owing money on a loan or credit card used to pay a medical bill, according to a 2025 study by researchers at the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.

Here's a look at more stories on how to manage, grow and protect your money for the years ahead.

Americans' credit card debt rose to a record $1.29 trillion in the third quarter, the New York Fed found, while home equity lines of credit saw their biggest quarterly jump since 2005, to $481 billion. Those increases come amid broader affordability pressures for consumers, including pricier everyday expenses amid persistent inflation.

It is "kind of hard to parse" how much of Americans' debt load comes from medical expenses, said Julie Margetta Morgan, president of The Century Foundation, a New York-based progressive nonprofit think tank.

"There's a big piece of medical debt that is about people being on high-deductible health plans, so they have insurance, but they're still getting these big medical bills that they can't pay," Margetta Morgan said.

Nationwide, among households with medical debt, the median amount in collections is $1,492, according to the Urban Institute. The nonprofit research organization's report drew the data from more than 10 million anonymized credit bureau records from August 2026.

Jessica Colon has used credit cards to pay medical debt. She and her husband were both self-employed at the time with a high-deductible health insurance plan. Colon, now 50, says the debt started with an unexpected $16,000 hospital bill for her son's care in the mid-2010s.

"Because I could barely keep up with that payment, I kept charging on my credit card all my monthly payments, and that's where it all compounded," Colon said. "I was just drowning."

The credit card debt grew to more than $45,000 before she turned to a nonprofit credit counselor, who negotiated lower rates and put together a five-year plan to pay it off. "It's embarrassing, honestly, how much money was interest, and then of course you're building the interest on the interest," Colon said. She zeroed out that debt in early 2024.

Before taking on debt, experts advise reviewing medical bills and billing codes for errors, including duplicate charges.

Consumers should also ask medical providers if they offer assistance before signing up for a medical credit card, experts say. Many nonprofit hospitals are required by law to have a written financial assistance plan.

"A lot of nonprofit hospitals are not actually offering this plan to people, and in many cases, they're offering them a medical credit card before they offer that financial assistance," said Margetta Morgan, a former associate director of research, monitoring and regulations at the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.

Colon said she wishes she had tried to negotiate the medical bills before putting the balance on a high-interest credit card. "You don't want to confront the place that's helping your child. So I didn't realize that I could do that," she said.

— CNBC's Sharon Epperson contributed reporting.

#117. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#115. Unions helped Democrats win big in 2026. Now they want action

When Brody Rutledge wasn't working 60-hour weeks or raising his four children this year, he was busy helping secure political victories for Democrats.

The 25-year-old was part of a group of Ohio union members who planned social-media blitzes in support of Amy Acton's campaign for governor and Sherrod Brown's effort to reclaim a Senate seat. On his lunch hour, during break times and after his kids went to sleep, the electrician helped create posts and videos to help raise the profiles of the two candidates among blue-collar voters.

The posts attracted more than 15,000 views.

Rutledge's efforts paid off: Acton and Brown won their respective races, part of the blue wave that swept Democrats to power in Washington and in state houses across America — including in many states, like Ohio, that had been considered Republican-leaning, or reliably Republican.

"People are realizing that there needs to be a change," Rutledge told CNBC. "We are extremely excited with the results."

Rutledge was a part of what organized labor considered one of its biggest political efforts yet. Unions spent millions of dollars and mobilized thousands of members to get out the vote for Democrats in the midterm election. Now, they expect newly elected politicians to deliver.

Democrats, they say, need to get a handle on inflation, enact worker protections and deliver union jobs.

The AFL-CIO launched a seven-figure paid media campaign for the election and said its members since Labor Day had engaged with nearly 15 million voters. By the AFL-CIO's count, more than 600 members of unions themselves were elected in the midterm, including dozens heading to Washington and hundreds who are bound for statehouses.

The CNBC Voter Poll shows midterm voters who were union members or lived in a union household went for Democrats over Republicans by a margin of 22 percentage points, the highest of the last four election cycles. That's more than double the margin for the rest of the electorate: When looking at voters in non-union households, the gap shrunk to 10 percentage points.

"We are very proud of the impact that the labor movement made on this election," said Liz Shuler, president of the AFL-CIO, a federation of unions that represents almost 15 million workers. "It was our biggest and boldest effort ever."

In some cases, unions got involved before party nominees had been decided.

In Michigan, the United Auto Workers organized behind Democrat Abdul El-Sayed ahead of what became a contentious primary race for U.S. Senate that he eventually won against Haley Stevens. The union believes that about one out of every 10 early voters in the August primary were UAW members.

"It was incredibly impactful," Connor Farrell, a progressive fundraiser who worked with El-Sayed, said of the UAW's endorsement. "I'm not sure we would have won without them."

El-Sayed and Jocelyn Benson — the Democratic nominee for Michigan governor, who also had early backing from the UAW — cruised to victory in last week's general election. It was a sharp reversal for the Wolverine state, which backed President Donald Trump in 2016 and 2024.

The UAW held a rally on the eve of Election Day headlined by El-Sayed and Benson, along with other Democrats running for statewide office. The Detroit-based union said its volunteers knocked on more than 100,000 doors and made half a million phone calls for the election.

The CNBC Voter Poll showed union households made up about 22% of the electorate in Michigan in the 2026 midterm, above the 16% mean nationwide. Union households in Michigan broke for El-Sayed and Benson by higher-than-average margins of 25 and 27 points, respectively.

"There's a direct correlation between a ballot box and a bread box," said UAW President Shawn Fain, who described the union's 2026 political efforts as the largest in his lifetime. "What we bargain for today can be taken away in the legislative halls tomorrow."

In Nevada, another swing state, members of AFL-CIO unions canvassed for a list of candidates including Aaron Ford, the Democrat who unseated Republican Gov. Joe Lombardo. Down ballot, the AFL-CIO endorsed candidates in state assembly, mayoral and city council races.

Vladimir Ortega, a member of the National Association of Letter Carriers living in North Las Vegas, said displeasure with how Republicans had managed the economy led him to volunteer to canvas with the AFL-CIO for the first time. The 34-year-old met Ford and other candidates at political events, and he plans to volunteer for candidates backed by AFL-CIO again in 2028.

"People felt like they had to be there," Ortega said. "Their money hurt, and they want to do something about it."

For generations, union voters and their families were a core bloc of the Democratic base. But the party's iron grip on organized labor had slipped in recent election cycles as union members, like other working-class voters, grew frustrated by high inflation and affordability challenges, according to Paul Clark, a Pennsylvania State University professor.

The National Election Pool's Poll showed union households voted for Democrats by a margin of 16 and 15 percentage points in the 2020 and 2022 contests, respectively. That narrowed to just 8 points in 2024, when voters' economic frustrations led Republicans to win a trifecta of power in Washington: the presidency and both houses of Congress.

The Teamsters declined to back either Trump or former Vice President Kamala Harris in 2024, snapping a long history of Democratic endorsements. The union, with more than one million members, said internal polling showed members preferred Trump over Harris.

Teamsters General President Sean O'Brien said on social media that he voted for Ashley Moody, the victorious Republican candidate for U.S. Senate in Florida, in the 2026 midterm. The Teamsters didn't respond to a CNBC request for comment.

"Obviously, 2024 was very disappointing for unions and the American labor movement," said Clark at Penn State, who has authored books on collective bargaining and organized labor. "That caused some introspection on the part of unions in terms of their political strategies and how they communicate with their members about politics."

This time around, there appeared to be more excitement among card-carrying union members heading to the polls. AFL-CIO polling showed 83% of union voters were "very motivated" to vote, an increase of 10 points from the last midterm election in 2022.

Following their midterm defeat, Clark said he wouldn't be surprised to see Republicans courting unions ahead of the 2028 presidential election. But that could be an uphill battle, given the party's history of advocating for business interests over workers, Clark said.

Looking ahead, organized labor has big expectations for Democrats after helping the party to power in Washington and state capitols across the country.

In Ohio, Rutledge said that his International Brotherhood of Electric Workers chapter is focused on ensuring new data center construction projects use union labor. In Michigan, UAW President Fain said the union expects a majority-Democrat state legislature to approve a law guaranteeing unemployment insurance for striking workers.

"The days of just blindly giving our endorsements to people because of party affiliation are over," Fain said. "They're going to earn our support."

If Democrats don't act when they have legislative majorities in Washington, union leaders warn that they will pull their support in 2028 and beyond.

Across the nation, Shuler, the AFL-CIO president, said union voters went to the polls last week in hopes of enacting policy that will level the economic playing field. The CNBC Voter Poll found that 70% of respondents believed the U.S. economy favors the wealthy over the middle- and working class.

"Republican, Democrat, independent, we'll be with you if you're with us," Shuler said. "It just so happens that we had a big wave of Democratic candidates who were talking about working-class issues."

#114. Nvidia's CEO was the rock star of AI before his dramatic unravelling

In recent years, Jensen Huang has been riding high on the crest of the AI wave as his company Nvidia became one of the most valuable in the world.

It only took a few days for it to all come crashing down.

Back-to-back revelations of two affairs involving the CEO, including a romantic relationship with an alleged Chinese spy, upended his seemingly untouchable status as a leading tech figure and led to his resignation from the company he helped form.

His departure leaves uncertainty in the global stock market, which leans on his microchip company, and over the future of a US company seen as a linchpin in the country's race for AI dominance over China.

On Wednesday, reports emerged that the 63-year-old married man was said to have had an affair with a Nvidia colleague, coming just days after revelations surfaced of a romantic relationship with a woman alleged to be a spy for China.

This hit the company's shares, and they sank again after the New York Times reported that Huang had had a second affair with an executive at his own company.

Huang is well known in the technology, business and political world, but despite his company's rocketing rise over the past four years, he perhaps isn't as big a household name as fellow tech bros Elon Musk, Jeff Bezos and Mark Zuckerburg.

However, Huang is probably more consequential to the development of AI across the world due to Nvidia making the best microchips in the business, which are used to power the technology.

The company, which is still valued at more than $4 trillion despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

This has led to Huang holding court with both US President Donald Trump and China's President XI Jinping. The leather-jacket wearing CEO has access to the world's powerful like few others.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn (Â£109bn), according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark black leather jacket look, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his current wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work at their father's firm.

He worked at US chip firm AMD before co-founding Nvidia. Known as "Daddy Huang" in Taiwan, he has a reputation as a workaholic with incredibly high standards for his staff.

The company became a key provider of computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in direction and entered the world of AI. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on the Jensen Huang, told the BBC, adding that he was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

The Nvidia co-founder has a famously close relationship with Trump. At a recent AI summit at the White House, where 42 tech executives were present, the president was flanked by Huang and Musk.

It was no surprise that Huang was at the top table. Companies using Nvidia's chips include Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic.

In essence, Huang's customer base includes the biggest firms on the planet and they are spending plenty on Nvidia's products.

This intertwining of companies means that the negative impact being felt by Nvidia, on the stock market for example, could perhaps have wider implications on other companies and the wider economy.

Witt told the BBC that Jensen Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, and that it was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," he added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, had backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

#113. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#110. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#108. Oil prices mixed after Trump says no Iran deal needed: 'We've got their Strait'

U.S. oil prices traded around the flatline Thursday, as comments from President Donald Trump stoked worries that an end to the U.S.-Iran war was not in sight.

Brent crude traded 0.5% higher at $93.29 per barrel at 9:09 a.m. West Texas Intermediate futures gained slipped 0.1% to trade at $86.05 per barrel.

"We don't need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed. We've got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we've got their Strait!" Trump said in a Truth Social post.

Thursday's gains follow a sharp pullback after Trump played down the prospect of further strikes on Iran after the midterm elections. WTI is down about 16% in November, according to data from FactSet.

On Sunday Trump said that fresh attacks were highly unlikely as talks to end the conflict progressed, despite both ⁠countries reporting that shipping had been hit in or near the Strait of Hormuz. On Monday, he said further military action did not appear necessary "at this point," easing concerns about prolonged disruptions to Middle East oil supplies.

John Kilduff, a partner at Again Capital, said investors may have been too quick to price in an end to the fighting.

"The market may have gotten ahead of itself on pricing in a cessation of hostilities," Kilduff told CNBC. He warned that renewed doubts about a deal would push the security premium in oil prices higher.

Trump's assertion that the U.S. controls the Strait of Hormuz could spur further Iranian attacks on shipping, increasing the risk of prolonged supply disruptions, according to Kilduff.

"The President may be thinking that given enough time, the economic blockade of Iran will force them to either negotiate with the USA or result in a regime change as the Iranian economy collapses. The Iranians might have different ideas and continue to attack Middle East Infrastructure that results in higher prices and forces the President to the negotiating table," said Andy Lipow, president of Lipow Oil Associates.

"In any event, it does not seem to me that either side's negotiating position has changed. Iran wants the blockade to end, sanctions to be lifted and a ceasefire including Lebanon to be declared. The USA wants Iran to get rid of its entire nuclear stockpile and allow free transit through the Strait of Hormuz," he said.

—CNBC's Pippa Stevens contributed to the report.

#104. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#102. Nebius falls 10% as company expects increased 2026 capex spend, issues weak outlook

Nebius shares sank 10% on Thursday as one of the biggest publicly traded neoclouds reported third-quarter revenue that beat analysts' estimates, but issued disappointing guidance.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

It was not immediately clear if the loss per share was comparable to the 69 cents expected by analysts.

Revenue jumped 571% in the quarter, according to a statement. The company reported a net loss of $37.5 million, a loss of 13 cents per share, compared with $119.6 million, a loss of 50 cents per share, a year earlier.

In a letter to shareholders, CEO Arkady Volozh said the company expects fiscal year 2026 revenue at the low end of the company's $3 billion to 3.4 billion range. FactSet expected full-year revenue of $3.35 billion.

Nebius also shared an initial outlook for 2027, expecting revenue of $12 billion to $13 billion, "reflecting a conservative approach to the pace of our build-out." FactSet expected 2027 revenue of $12.09 billion.

The company expects FY2026 capital expenditures of $27 billion, higher than its previous capex guidance of $20 billion to $25 billion and the FactSet expectation of $23.85 billion.

Nebius' expected FY2027 capex of $40 billion to $45 billion came in much higher than the FactSet expectation of $28.77 billion.

Nebius' results come after fellow neoclouds CoreWeave and IREN reported better-than-expected financials in their earnings announcements over the past week. That's provided a boost to the companies' stocks, which had suffered steep selloffs in recent weeks on concern about the sustainability of the artificial intelligence buildout.

The neocloud companies posted a robust rally on Wednesday, with Coreweave booming nearly 25%, Nebius climbing 15%, Applied Digital rising more than 7% and IREN adding nearly 6%.

Each of the stocks fell Thursday premarket after the Nebius Q3 report.

Last week, CoreWeave's bond yields spiked past the 16% mark. In its earnings report on Tuesday, CoreWeave told analysts it expects to keep parent-level debt financing under $5 billion through 2027.

The political backdrop has created further volatility as Nebius races rivals to open up new data centers. Last week U.S. voters supported dozens of ballot initiatives to restrict the development of such facilities, while Democrats won sweeping victories in both houses of Congress.

Then there's the Nvidia problem. Following stinging revelations about CEO Jensen Huang's affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, the Wall Street Journal reported late Wednesday that Nvidia's board was planning to push out the company's co-founder.

Nvidia, which has developed the neocloud market by investing in the companies and backstopping some of their projects, held about $329 million in Nebius shares at the end of June, according to a filing.

During the quarter, Nebius said it raised $5.75 billion in debt financing.

Executives will discuss the results with analysts and issue guidance on a conference call starting at 8 a.m. ET.

#100. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant in South Carolina, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia, South Carolina. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12-to-18 months "would be great" and something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said the EREVs and SUV are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exterior appearances that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

#94. Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Company that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalise said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalise said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.

#93. Jensen and Lori Huang's Nvidia fortune is jointly controlled. How that makes things complicated now that he's resigned

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions straight to your inbox.

Jensen Huang's $140 billion stake in Nvidia is largely held by trusts that are co-owned with his wife, Lori, creating added uncertainty for the shares and his financial future following his resignation from the company, according to attorneys and SEC filings.

Huang and his wife jointly own about 800 million Nvidia shares worth roughly $135 billion through a complex web of trusts and LLCs created for tax and estate planning purposes, according to SEC filings. Jensen Huang directly owns about 70 million shares valued at about $12 billion.

While owning company stock through a trust is common among tech founders and CEOs, it's less typical for a founder to have equal ownership with a spouse, tax experts say. The arrangement further complicates Huang's sudden exit from the company, but could prove to be a stabilizing force for the shares, since the trusts make it more difficult for either Huang or his wife to quickly sell.

"Each of Mr. Huang and his wife may be deemed to have shared beneficial ownership of, and to exercise shared voting and investment power over," according to an SEC filing.

According to the filings, Jensen and Lori Huang own about 530 million shares as co-trustees of the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Living Trust, created in 1995. They're also co-trustees of the Huang 2012 Irrevocable Trust, which owns 31.4 million shares, and the Huang Irrevocable Remainder Trust, which owns 109 million shares. About 135 million shares are held by LLCs that have the Living Trust as the sole member, according to the filings.

Lori Huang is the president of the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation, which owns 69 million shares and reported $9 billion in net assets in 2024, according to IRS filings.

Each of the trust structures serves different tax or estate purposes, according to trust and estate attorneys. The living trust is likely a revocable marital trust, since it was created 10 years after the Huangs married and two years after Nvidia was founded in 1993.  Attorneys say living trusts help couples avoid the potentially expensive and public probate process  when one spouse dies. Many couples, for example, create living trusts for homes they purchase or other assets.

The main tax benefit of living trusts is that when one spouse dies, the surviving spouse gets a step-up in basis for the entire trust's assets, rather than just their half, according to attorneys.

While the rules of each living trust vary, attorneys say shares in the living trust can typically be sold or divided fairly easily in the case of a divorce.

"It's like a joint checking account," said Kathleen Martone, partner at Varnum LLP.

While the revocable trust would typically allow each of them to sell their shares, lawyers said it is common for executives or their trusts to have agreements with the company limiting the sale or transfer of shares to prevent large-scale liquidation.

"In the event they divide shares in their revocable trust, there's probably transfer restrictions. He may have to hold them for her benefit, and she would get the monetary benefit of the shares but not voting or management rights," said Leslie Evans, partner at Withers. "He could retain the stock while Lori receives other assets or an equalization payment."

The more complicated structures used by the Huangs, and the ones with the largest tax benefits, are the irrevocable trusts. The SEC filings state that "Mr Huang and his wife are co-trustees" of both trusts. With an irrevocable trust, the creator transfers ownership of an asset into the trust and the terms can't be changed without the consent of the beneficiaries or a court.

Attorneys say the 2012 irrevocable trust is likely an "intentionally defective grantor trust," a type of irrevocable trust where the creator gifts assets but is still treated by the IRS as the owner of the property for income tax purposes.

The structure allows the creator to lend money to the trust to buy the shares, as well as pay income taxes for the trust when it earns money. The result — an unlimited gift to the trust that escapes the estate tax.

What's more, the trust can avoid capital gains taxes on any appreciation of the shares in the trust.

"The trust principal is staying high, my own taxable personal assets are being used to pay the tax, and I'm able to funnel more and more money into the trust," said Azriel Baer, trust and estates partner at Farrell Fritz, P.C. in New York.

Their other irrevocable trust, the "Huang Irrevocable Remainder Trust," was likely created for the benefit of their children.

By definition, the trustees and terms of an irrevocable trust are difficult to change. Since Jensen Huang and Lori Huang are both co-trustees, neither can likely remove or sell the Nvidia shares held by the trust, experts say.

"An irrevocable trust is its own entity that has its own operational provisions, and so it has some estate and asset protection planning," Martone said. "Theoretically an irrevocable trust would be protected from a divorcing spouse as a creditor."

Of course, the protections all depend on the terms of the trust, attorneys say. Whether an irrevocable trust can be changed or "decanted" in legal parlance depends in part on state law. California, where the Huangs live, is strict when it comes to changing irrevocable trusts.

For Nvidia shareholders, the irrevocable trusts could provide stability for the shares, since it would be difficult for either Jensen or Lori Huang to remove the shares from the trust and sell them.

Attorneys say it's unlikely the Huangs would change a trust created for the benefit of their children.

"If (the trusts) are for benefit for kids, there's no reason to pull them back into their estate," said Rochelle Schultz, shareholder at Weinstock Manion.
